ParlaMint-ES-GA_2021-12-09-DSPG060.u143	To formulate the non-law proposition, Mr. Aranguena has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2021-12-09-DSPG060.u144	Thank you, Mr. President. For an important part of the problems that accumulate to humanity today — which are not few or paint too well — are derived from the fact that we are part of an almost completely denaturalized civilization that lives in its majority in urban spaces deprived of any contact with nature; a nature that is alien to us, when not hostile, even though we are part of it, because we are nature as much as we are culture. More specifically, naturalist Joaquín Araújo says — a very graphic expression — "that we are a forest that has walked." A forest that has walked means that the apes we descended from and with which we are closely related have developed much of their skills and abilities raised in the trees that served them as shelter against predators, in close relation to those forests in which they lived. But it also refers to the importance of forests as the livelihood of which we are part, because without forests there would be no life. That's why it's hard to overstate the importance, the enormous importance that forests have for our survival in a world of accelerated ecological collapse. In close connection with all of the above, one of the agreements adopted at the recent COP26 — in fact, the first major agreement reached at that conference — was to curb deforestation, at least to try to curb the deforestation of the planet, on the basis of the consensus that forests are the most important, cheap and effective instrument that humanity has to fight against the climate crisis, which represents the greatest ecological threat, but also existential, economic and social threats that humanity has never faced, questioning and questioning all and every aspect of our lives, as well as our civilization as a whole. Because forests are part of or form one of the sinks, the largest carbon sinks on the planet. And on the other hand, the destruction of forests produced by climate change implies the release into the atmosphere of an ingenious amount of CO2, not to mention the CO2 that emits the destruction of forests directly caused by human activity. There will be those who say what problem we have in Galicia with the forests, if trees are left, there are many trees, we produce half the wood that is cut down in Spain. But for ecological purposes, we have to distinguish clearly, because there is a huge, abysmal difference between the concept of forest and the concept of forest planting. As the Ministry for Ecological Transition points out in its report Forests and biodiversity in the face of climate change, monospecific forest masses of alloctone species, such as eucalyptus, increase forest vulnerability in the face of global warming, while the most biodiversity-based structures of native species are shown to be much more resilient. Ecosystem services provided by forests are enormous and, compared to any other ecosystem. In addition to absorbing CO2, they fix and stabilize the ground, they are the largest terrestrial refuge in biodiversity because they host and feed all kinds of living organisms — such as animals, plants and fungi of all kinds — through ecosystems that generate around them, contribute to creating a climate more conducive to life and humanity by their ability to attract rain, and a very long, very long -- I would say, etc. We must also highlight the landscape benefits of the native forest, which adorn the landscape, especially by the variety of colors that occur in autumn and, to a lesser extent, in spring, with a landscape quality that obviously do not have eucalyptus plantations. Then there are social benefits too, because forests allow us to come into contact with a natural environment or simply disconnect from the stress of our everyday lives. It plays here, here comes the concept of biophila, which implies that, as animals that evolve our genetic baggage in close contact with nature, we need that contact -- although we don't realize it -- because that contact improves our physical and soul well-being, and its absence makes us worse. One of the countries with the largest forest surface and forest tradition and culture in the world as Japan officially recognizes and promotes in recent decades a practice called shinrin yoku, which means "absorbing the atmosphere of the forest"—literally—, moving to our country in the concept of "forest washrooms," a practice supported by studies that demonstrate that these forest baths have distinctly positive physical and psychological effects. For all of the above, and much more, the native forest, as a more biodiverse terrestrial ecosystem, which provides a greater amount of ecosystem services, quantity and variety, should be weighed on the public policies of a civilized country in the 21st century, in line with the constitutional right to a suitable environment for human development, because there can be no suitable environment if there is no forest, to the point that it would not be exaggerated to speak as a correlation of that right to the environment of a hypothetical right to forest, from which much of the European citizenship — which lives on the coast and particularly in large urban areas — is being deprived of the difficulty of finding a forest in a minimally suitable conservation state in its immediate surroundings, which goes against not only the Constitution but also the European Green Pact, of the European Forest Strategy for the year 2030, adopted in July 2030. Because Galicia, despite having a large forest surface, is losing its forests in a much accelerated way in much of its territory; a fact that does not adequately reflect the ups and downs — much more partial — official statistics, but perfectly visible to anyone who goes around so many areas on the roadside the remains of the last strawberries converted into logs of cut trunks parallel to the growth of uncontrolled eucalyptus nitens plantations, which proliferate more and more towards inside Galicia. Because the nitens, as we know, breaks the geographic barrier that the eucalyptus globulus had and therefore allows colonization of the rest of Galician territory that was not yet colonized by the eucalyptus. Little remains of that enticing universe that Wenceslao Fernández Flórez told, that lively forest, where animals, men, legends coexisted, and, in short, an ancestral culture rooted in the land that was the traditional culture of Galicia. Sadly, those who take a turn around Cecebre can verify that the animated forest no longer exists in reality, out of some libraries in the form of novel—new it, by the way, highly recommended. But today the forest sector accounts for more than 3% of Galicia's GDP and one of the main economic activities, which takes up tens of thousands of people in an unemployment-stricken country; that's a reality. By itself Galicia is the ninth European forest power, it produces half of the wood that is cut down in Spain. Those magnitudes have come to an end despite the public's desire that one third of the Galician mountain is unproductive, much higher than other places with forest tradition, which gives an idea of the great or enormous unsavory potential. And the 1992 Forest Plan intended to invest in the sector 3% of the autonomous budget and in practice that budget fell in half, spending more on extinguishing fires than rationalizing the mountain, which would involve diversifying forest species and also the uses of the mountain, distinguishing between economic, social and environmental, and, by course, diversifying economics as well. In summary, the only forest policy that the successive popular governments practiced was that of "you going to plant", basically eucalyptus, so that the goal of the 1992 Plan for 2032, 245,000 hectares of eucalyptus, is estimated more than doubled today, not by official statistics but by the most exact ones that are not so official, with a minimum of 500,000 hectares. But the rule of law, like democracy itself, is a good measure to practice balance. They are balanced both the rule of law and democracy: to balance values, rights and interests often opposed. And that's why the economic value of eucalyptus must inevitably be compatible with the environmental and social value of the indigenous forest, protected by the constitutional right to an adequate environment. This can only be done by a determined public intervention, which is not taking place today. [[Applause]] And in that sense, in addition to the change in forestry policy that the Socialist Group has so often claimed, the creation of a public network of native forests would help improve our landscape, create spaces for sprawling and enjoy nature and produce a great environmental and social benefit with very little investment of public resources. Because planting trees or preserving existing ones is much cheaper than creating cement-based infrastructures. The native forest can also serve as a school forest or a school of nature, as is done in many countries of the world, to a lesser extent in Spain. To create a public network of native forests would therefore be a public initiative of maximum effectiveness, efficiency and effectiveness. So if you will vote against it, I hope you will give some reasonable explanation that this initiative is not part of the Socialist Group. And by the way, I am sure that this initiative would count on the support and collaboration of all socialist councils and deputies, in which most of Galicians live. Nothing else, and we're going on.
